Find the sum of all integer bases $b>9$ for which $17_{b}$ is a divisor of $97_{b}$.
Please reason step by step, and put your final answer within \boxed{}.
assistant 
We need integer base b>9, where this means digits possible 0-…(b-1). 17_b is a divisor (exactly divides) 97_b. We need to express numbers in base 10: 17_b = 1*b +7 = b+7. 97_b = 9*b +7. We want (b+7) divides (9b+7). Let d = b+7. Then 9b+7 = 9(b) +7. Express in terms of d: b = d -7. Then 9b+7 = 9(d-7)+7 = 9d -63 +7 = 9d -56. Want d divides 9d -56. Equivalent to d divides 56? Because 9d -56 ≡ -56 (mod d), so d | 56. So d must be a positive divisor of 56 such that d = b+7 >? Also base >9, so b>9, hence d = b+7>16. Also digits in base: 9 is a digit allowed? In base b>9, digits 0- (b-1) include 9 if b>10 actually? Wait base > 9 means b≥10. For base 10, digit 9 is allowed. For base 9, digit 9 not allowed. So base must be >9. So b≥10. That's fine.

Now d = b+7 will be at least 10+7 =17. So d > 16.

Also note that b must also satisfy b>9 and 9 must be a legal digit, meaning b>9 ensures digit 9 allowed. Also 7 digit obviously allowed if b>7? B≥10 ensures both digits allowed. So fine.

So we need positive integer divisors d of 56 that are >16. Let's compute divisors of 56: 1,2,4,7,8,14,28,56. Those >16: 28 and 56. So potential d = 28 or 56. Then b = d -7. For d=28, b = 21. b>9? Yes. For d=56, b = 49. b>9? yes.

But we must also check that the base digits used (9 and 7) are valid: for base 21 digits 0-20, includes 9 and 7, yes. For base 49 digits 0-48, includes 9 and 7, yes. Also condition b>9 satisfied. Also we should check that 17_b is divisor of 97_b. Let's confirm: For b=21: 17_b = 21+7=28. 97_b = 9*21+7 = 189+7 = 196. Does 28 divide 196? 28*7=196. Yes.

For b=49: 17_b=49+7=56. 97_b=9*49+7=441+7=448. 56 divides 448? 56*8=448. Yes.

Thus both bases work. Sum of all integer bases >9: 21+49=70. So answer: 70.

But let's double-check if there are other possible d: 56 factors 1,2,4,7,8,14,28,56. Only 28 and 56 >16. Good.

Thus solution: 70. Ensure to put within boxed.

Thus final answer: \boxed{70}.

assistant 
To work in base \(b\) we translate the numbers to base \(10\).

\[
17_b = 1\cdot b+7 = b+7,\qquad 
97_b = 9\cdot b+7 = 9b+7.
\]

We need \(b+7\) to divide \(9b+7\).

Let \(d=b+7\). Then \(b=d-7\) and

\[
9b+7 = 9(d-7)+7 = 9d-56.
\]

Hence, \(d \mid 9d-56 \iff d \mid 56\).

Thus, \(d\) must be a positive divisor of \(56\).  
The divisors of \(56\) are \(1,2,4,7,8,14,28,56\).

Because \(b>9\), we have \(d=b+7>16\).  
The only divisors of \(56\) larger than \(16\) are \(28\) and \(56\).

* \(d=28\): \(b=28-7=21\).
  \[
  17_{21}=28,\quad 97_{21}=9\cdot21+7=196,\quad 28\mid196.
  \]
* \(d=56\): \(b=56-7=49\).
  \[
  17_{49}=56,\quad 97_{49}=9\cdot49+7=448,\quad 56\mid448.
  \]

Both bases satisfy the condition.  
The sum of all required integer bases is

\[
21+49=70.
\]

\[
\boxed{70}
\]

Sampled message

assistant 
Let  

\[
A=(0,0),\qquad B=(28,0)
\]

and let the vector \( \overrightarrow{AC}= (x,\;y)\) with \(|AC|=91\).

\[
C=(x,\;y),\qquad |AC|^2=x^2+y^2=91^{2}=8281
\]

--------------------------------------------------------------------
### 1.  Coordinates of the other points  

\[
\begin{aligned}
D &= (4,0),&\qquad\ &E &= (20,0),\\[2mm]
F &= \left(\frac{13}{91}x,\ \frac{13}{91}y\right),&
\qquad G &=\left(\frac{65}{91}x,\ \frac{65}{91}y\right).
\end{aligned}
\]

\[
M=2F-D=\left(\frac{26}{91}x-4,\ \frac{26}{91}y\right),\qquad
N=2E-G=\left(40-\frac{65}{91}x,\ -\frac{65}{91}y\right).
\]

--------------------------------------------------------------------
### 2.  Find \(y\) from the given area of \(DEGF\)

Using the shoelace formula for \(D\,(4,0),\,E\,(20,0),\,G\,(g_x,g_y),\,F\,(f_x,f_y)\) :

\[
\begin{aligned}
[DEGF]&=\frac12\Bigl(
20\,g_y+g_x f_y-g_y f_x-4f_y\Bigr) \\[2mm]
      &=\frac12\Bigl(
20\,g_y-4f_y\Bigr)            \qquad(\text{because }g,f\text{ are collinear with }A)\\[2mm]
      &=\frac12\left(
20\frac{65}{91}y-4\frac{13}{91}y\right)
      =\frac{1248}{91}\,y.
\end{aligned}
\]

Given \([DEGF]=288\),

\[
\frac{1248}{91}\,y=288\;\Longrightarrow\;
y=\frac{288\cdot91}{1248}=42.
\]

--------------------------------------------------------------------
### 3.  Area of \(\triangle ABC\)

With base \(|AB|=28\) and height \(y=42\),

\[
[ABC]=\tfrac12\cdot 28\cdot 42=588.
\]

--------------------------------------------------------------------
### 4.  Area of the heptagon \(AFNBCEM\)

Apply shoelace to the ordered vertices

\[
A,\;F,\;N,\;B,\;C,\;E,\;M .
\]

The sum of the cross‑products simplifies (using the expressions for the
points) to

\[
[AFNBCEM]=\frac12\,(2\cdot14\,y)=14\,y.
\]

Since \(y=42\),

\[
[AFNBCEM]=14\cdot42=588.
\]

--------------------------------------------------------------------
\[
\boxed{588}
\]

The 9 members of a baseball team went to an ice cream parlor after their game. Each player had a singlescoop cone of chocolate, vanilla, or strawberry ice cream. At least one player chose each flavor, and the number of players who chose chocolate was greater than the number of players who chose vanilla, which was greater than the number of players who chose strawberry. Let $N$ be the number of different assignments of flavors to players that meet these conditions. Find the remainder when $N$ is divided by 1000.
Please reason step by step, and put your final answer within \boxed{}.
assistant 
We have a problem: 9 team members; each chooses one of 3 flavors. Must have at least one of each flavor. Counts constraint: #chocolate > #vanilla > #strawberry. Let c, v, s be counts, integer >=1, sum =9. Also inequality c > v > s. Count number of assignments (distinguishable players) given counts. For each valid triple (c,v,s) we need number of ways to assign: multinomial coefficient 9!/(c! v! s!). Then sum over all triples satisfying constraints.

Compute all triples (c,v,s) positive integers summing to 9, with c > v > s.

We can enumerate possibilities. Let's do systematically.

Let s smallest.

We need v > s, c > v. Let's parameterize with distinct positive integers.

We can list triples of distinct positive integers summing to 9 with strict decreasing order: c>v>s, each >=1. Let's list all partitions into 3 distinct positive parts.

List partitions of 9 into 3 distinct parts (unordered). Then we can assign order c>v>s. Let's find all unordered triples.

We need p1 + p2 + p3 = 9, distinct positives.

Possible sets:

Try smallest s=1. Then we need c>v>1, c+v = 8. Distinct pairs >1 and <8.

Pairs of distinct integers >1 summing to 8: possibilities: (2,6), (3,5). 4+4 invalid because not distinct. So sets {6,2,1} and {5,3,1}. Order c>v: For set {6,2,1}, c=6, v=2, s=1. For set {5,3,1}, c=5, v=3, s=1.

Next s=2. Then need v > 2, c > v, c+v=7. Distinct >2. Options: (3,4). (5,? 2) no because >2. So only (3,4). So set {4,3,2}. Order c=4, v=3, s=2.

s=3. Then need v > 3, c > v, c+v=6. Distinct >3. Options: (4,2)? 2 not >3. (5,1) no. So none.

s=4 would yield remaining sum 5 with v >4, c>v, min v=5, then c=0 none.

So only 3 unordered triples. So we have exactly 3 possible (c,v,s) assignments:

1. (6,2,1)
2. (5,3,1)
3. (4,3,2)

Now compute multinomials.

Compute 9! = 362880.

Case 1: 9!/(6! 2! 1!) = 362880 / (720 * 2 * 1) = 362880 / 1440 = 252. Let's compute: 362880/1440 = 252.

Case 2: 9!/(5! 3! 1!) = 362880 / (120 * 6 * 1) = 362880 / 720 = 504.

Case 3: 9!/(4! 3! 2!) = 362880 / (24 * 6 * 2) = 362880 / (288) = 1260? Let's compute: 24*6=144, *2=288. 362880 / 288 = 1260 (since 288*1000=288000, diff 74880; 288*260=74880; so 1260). Yes.

Sum: 252 + 504 + 1260 = 2016. Check sum: 252+504=756; 756+1260=2016.

Now answer modulo 1000: 2016 mod 1000 = 16. So remainder 16.

We need to put result in \boxed{16}.

Let's also double-check enumeration.

Is there any triple like (7,1,1)? No because distinct, but start condition: at least one of each flavor, but not necessarily distinct counts? Wait inequality says c > v > s, so they must be strictly ordered, so cannot have equal counts. So counts must be distinct. So enumeration above fine.

Double-check that there are no other triples: Let's list all possibilities for s=1: pair sums 8 with distinct >1: Options enumerated: (2,6); (3,5). (4,4) doesn't satisfy v> s? Actually v would be 4, s=1, but then c would be 4? Wait if we had (4,4) but counts must be distinct. So not allowed. So only 2.

s=2: sum 7 distinct >2: pairs (3,4). (1,6) invalid cause 1 not >2, (2,5) invalid because equal to s. So only 1.

s=3: need sum 6 with distinct >3: possibilities (4,2) invalid, (5,1) invalid, (6,0) invalid. None.

So total 3 distinct triples.

Compute each combinatorial count:

Case (6,2,1): 9!/(6!2!1!) = 362880/(720*2) = 362880/1440=252 yes.

Case (5,3,1): 9!/(5!3!1!) = 362880/(120*6)=362880/720=504 correct.

Case (4,3,2): 9!/(4!3!2!) = 362880/(24*6*2=288) => 1260 yes.

Sum 252+504=756 and 756+1260=2016.

2016 mod 1000 = 16.

Thus final answer: \boxed{16}.

assistant 
First let  

\[
c=\text{# of chocolate scoops},\qquad 
v=\text{# of vanilla scoops},\qquad 
s=\text{# of strawberry scoops}.
\]

We need  

\[
c+v+s=9,\qquad c>v>s,\qquad c,v,s\ge 1 .
\]

--------------------------------------------------------------------
### 1.  Find all admissible triples \((c,v,s)\)

Since the numbers are distinct, we look for the partitions of 9 into three
different positive parts.

* \(s=1\)  

  \(c+v=8\).  
  Distinct pairs \((c,v)\) with \(v>1\) are \((6,2)\) and \((5,3)\).  
  So we get \((6,2,1)\) and \((5,3,1)\).

* \(s=2\)  

  \(c+v=7\).  
  The only distinct pair with \(v>2\) is \((4,3)\).  
  So we get \((4,3,2)\).

* \(s\ge 3\) gives no solutions because \(c+v\) would be too small for two distinct integers \(>s\).

Hence the only possible triples are  

\[
(6,2,1),\quad (5,3,1),\quad (4,3,2).
\]

--------------------------------------------------------------------
### 2.  Count assignments for each triple

For a given triple \((c,v,s)\) the number of ways to assign the flavors to 9
distinct players is the multinomial coefficient  

\[
\frac{9!}{c!\,v!\,s!}.
\]

* \((6,2,1)\): \(\displaystyle \frac{9!}{6!\,2!\,1!}= \frac{362880}{720\cdot 2}=252\).

* \((5,3,1)\): \(\displaystyle \frac{9!}{5!\,3!\,1!}= \frac{362880}{120\cdot 6}=504\).

* \((4,3,2)\): \(\displaystyle \frac{9!}{4!\,3!\,2!}= \frac{362880}{24\cdot 6\cdot 2}=1260\).

--------------------------------------------------------------------
### 3.  Sum the counts

\[
N = 252 + 504 + 1260 = 2016.
\]

--------------------------------------------------------------------
### 4.  Remainder modulo 1000

\[
2016 \bmod 1000 = 16.
\]

--------------------------------------------------------------------
\[
\boxed{16}
\]

### 1.  Solve for the ratio \(t=\frac{x}{y}\)

\[

\]

Let \(t=\dfrac{x}{y}\).

\[

\]

Its discriminant is  

\[
\Delta = (-1)^2-4\cdot 12\cdot(-6)=1+288=289=17^{2}.
\]

Hence

\[
t=\frac{1\pm17}{24}=\frac{18}{24}= \frac34 
\qquad\text{or}\qquad
t=\frac{-16}{24}= -\frac23 .
\]

So, if \(y\neq 0\),

\[
\frac{x}{y}=\frac34\quad\text{or}\quad\frac{x}{y}=-\frac23 .
\]

This gives the two parametric families

\[
\begin{cases}
x=\frac34\,y      &\Longrightarrow (x,y)=(3k,\,4k),\\[2pt]
x=-\frac23\,y     &\Longrightarrow (x,y)=(-2k',\,3k')
\end{cases}

where \(k,k'\) are integers.

assistant 
To be divisible by \(22\) an integer must be divisible by both \(2\) and \(11\).

---

### 1.  Condition for divisibility by \(2\)

The last digit must be even.  
From the digits \(\{1,2,3,4,5,6,7,8\}\) the even digits are\(\{2,4,6,8\}\).

So there are \(4\) possible choices for the unit digit.

---

### 2.  Condition for divisibility by \(11\)

For an \(8\)-digit number the rule for \(11\) says that  

\[
(\text{sum of digits in odd positions})-(\text{sum of digits in even positions})
\]

must be a multiple of \(11\).

Let \(S_{\text{odd}}\) be the sum of the four digits in the odd positions
(positions 1, 3, 5, 7).  
Let \(S_{\text{even}}\) be the sum of the four digits in the even positions
(positions 2, 4, 6, 8).

All digits are \(1\)–\(8\), so  

\[
S_{\text{odd}}+S_{\text{even}} = 1+2+\cdots+8 = 36 .
\]

Thus  

\[
S_{\text{odd}}-S_{\text{even}}
=
S_{\text{odd}}-(36-S_{\text{odd}})
=2S_{\text{odd}}-36 .
\]

We need \(2S_{\text{odd}}-36 \equiv 0 \pmod{11}\).

\(36\equiv3\pmod{11}\), so  

\[
2S_{\text{odd}}\equiv 3 \pmod{11}\quad\Longrightarrow\quad 
S_{\text{odd}}\equiv 3\cdot 6\equiv18\pmod{11}.
\]

The sum of four distinct digits chosen from \(1\!-\!8\) ranges from  
\(1+2+3+4=10\) to \(5+6+7+8=26\).  
Only

\[
S_{\text{odd}}=18
\]

is possible.  
Hence the four odd‑position digits must exactly add up to \(18\); the four
even‑position digits automatically sum to \(18\) as well.

---

### 3.  Counting arrangements

**Choose the unit digit**

There are \(4\) choices (\(2,4,6,8\)).

**Choose the four odd‑position digits**

Remove the chosen unit digit.  
We have \(7\) digits left and must pick \(4\) of them whose sum is \(18\).

For *each* even unit digit there are \(4\) such 4‑digit subsets
(the same count for every choice of the unit digit, because the set of
remaining digits differs only by one element, yet the sum condition is
symmetric).  

Each subset can be arranged in the odd positions in \(4!\) ways,
and the remaining \(3\) digits are placed in the even positions
\(2,4,6\) in \(3!\) ways.

Thus for a fixed unit digit we have

\[
\underbrace{4}_{\text{subsets}}\times
\underbrace{4!}_{\text{odd positions}}\times
\underbrace{3!}_{\text{even positions}}
=
4\times24\times6
=576
\]

valid numbers.

Since there are \(4\) possible unit digits,

\[
N = 4 \times 576 = 2304 .
\]

---

N - 2025 = 2304 - 2025 = 279 .
\]

\boxed{279}

Because cross terms cancel.

Sampled message

assistant 
For a tangential quadrilateral the incircle is tangent to all four sides.  
In a trapezoid that is also isosceles we can use this property together with the
height.

---

### 1.  Height of the trapezoid  

The circle touches both parallel sides, so the centre of the circle is
equidistant from the two bases.  
Hence the distance between the bases (the height \(h\)) equals twice the
radius:

\[
h=2\cdot3=6 .
\]

---

### 2.  Relation between the bases  

Area of a trapezoid:

\[
A=\frac{(r+s)}{2}\,h .
\]

Given \(A=72\) and \(h=6\),

\[
72=\frac{(r+s)}{2}\cdot 6 \quad\Longrightarrow\quad r+s=24. \tag{1}
\]

---

### 3.  Length of the legs  

For a tangential quadrilateral the sum of the lengths of opposite sides are
equal.  
In our trapezoid the opposite sides are a base and a leg, so

\[
r+s= \text{leg}_1+\text{leg}_2 = 2\,\text{leg}.
\]

Thus the equal legs have length

\[
\text{leg}= \frac{r+s}{2}=12. \tag{2}
\]

---

### 4.  Horizontal shift between the bases  

Let the shift on each side be \(x\).  
The leg, height, and shift form a right triangle:

\[
\text{leg}^{\,2}=h^{\,2}+x^{\,2}\quad\Longrightarrow\quad
12^{2}=6^{2}+x^{2}\;\Longrightarrow\;x^2=108\;\Longrightarrow\;x=6\sqrt3.
\]

The difference of the bases satisfies

\[
\frac{s-r}{2}=x=6\sqrt3 \;\Longrightarrow\; s-r=12\sqrt3. \tag{3}
\]

---

### 5.  Solve for \(r\) and \(s\)

From (1) and (3):

\[
\begin{cases}
s+r = 24,\\
s-r = 12\sqrt3.
\end{cases}
\]

Adding and subtracting:

\[
\begin{aligned}
2s &= 24+12\sqrt3 \;\Longrightarrow\; s = 12+6\sqrt3,\\[2pt]
2r &= 24-12\sqrt3 \;\Longrightarrow\; r = 12-6\sqrt3.
\end{aligned}
\]

---

### 6.  Compute \(r^{2}+s^{2}\)

Let \(a=12\) and \(b=6\sqrt3\).  
Then \(r=a-b,\; s=a+b\).  
Hence

\[
r^{2}+s^{2}=(a-b)^{2}+(a+b)^{2}=2(a^{2}+b^{2}).
\]

Now

\[
a^{2}=144,\qquad b^{2}=(6\sqrt3)^{2}=108,
\]
\[
a^{2}+b^{2}=144+108=252,
\]
\[
r^{2}+s^{2}=2\cdot252=504.
\]

---

\[
\boxed{504}
\]

For example, a possible result is $AB,CJ,DG,EK,FL,HI$. The probability that the last word listed contains $G$ is $\frac{m}{n}$, where $m$ and $n$ are relatively prime positive integers. Find $m+n$.

assistant

assistant 
The 12 letters  

\[
A,B,C,D,E,F,G,H,I,J,K,L
\]

are paired at random.  
For every pair we write the two letters in alphabetical order and the six
two‑letter words are then arranged alphabetically.  
We need the probability that the very last word in that alphabetical list
contains the letter \(G\).

--------------------------------------------------------------------

### 1.  How can a word containing \(G\) become last?

The last word is the one whose first letter is **the largest** of the six
first letters produced by the pairs.  
Let the letter paired with \(G\) be \(X\).

* If \(X>G\) (for instance \(H,I,J,K\) or \(L\)), the word containing \(G\)
  is \(GX\); here \(G\) is the first letter.
  For this word to be last, no other first letter may exceed \(G\).
  Therefore **every letter larger than \(G\) must be the second letter of some pair**.

* If \(X<G\) (for instance \(A,B,C,D,E\) or \(F\)), the word is \(XG\); here
  \(G\) is the second letter.
  For the last word to contain \(G\), the first letter \(X\) must be the
  largest of the six first letters, i.e. **no first letter may exceed \(X\)**.

--------------------------------------------------------------------

### 2.  Counting matchings

---

#### a) \(X>G\)  (there are 5 possibilities \(H,I,J,K,L\))

After fixing the pair \(G\,X\) the remaining letters are  

* the six small letters \(\{A,B,C,D,E,F\}\),
* the four big letters \(\{H,I,J,K,L\}\setminus\{X\}\).

All four big letters have to be paired with distinct small letters.
Choose which 4 small letters they get and pair them:

\[
\binom{6}{4}\times 4! = 15\times 24 = 360
\]

matchings for each choice of \(X\).

Hence

\[
5\times 360 = 1800\text{ matchings.}
\]

---

#### b) \(X<F\)

If \(X\le F\) and \(X<G\), then the set of letters larger than \(X\) but not
\(G\) has at least 5 members:
\(\{F,G,H,I,J,K,L\}\) (when \(X=E\)) or more.  
These 5 or more large letters would all have to be second letters, but only
\(4\) small letters (\(A,B,C,D\)) are available to pair with them, which is
impossible.  
Therefore **no matchings exist** when \(X\) is \(A,B,C\) or \(D\).

---

#### c) \(X=F\)

Now the remaining letters are  

* small letters \(\{A,B,C,D,E\}\),
* big letters \(\{H,I,J,K,L\}\).

Each of the five big letters must be paired with one of the five small
letters.  The number of bijections is

\[
5! = 120 .
\]

Thus we have 120 matchings for \(X=F\).

--------------------------------------------------------------------

### 3.  Total favorable matchings

\[
\text{Favourable} = 120 + 5\times 360 = 1920 .
\]

The total number of possible pairings of 12 distinct letters into 6
unordered pairs is

\[
\frac{12!}{2^6\,6!}=10395 .
\]

--------------------------------------------------------------------

### 4.  Probability

\[
P=\frac{1920}{10395}=\frac{128}{693}\quad(\text{reduced form}).
\]

Hence \(m=128\) and \(n=693\), and

\[
m+n=128+693=821 .
\]

--------------------------------------------------------------------

\[
\boxed{821}
\]

The equations

\[
|25+20i-z|=5 ,\qquad |z-(4+k)|=|z-(k+3i)|
\]

express that \(z\) is on a circle centered at \(25+20i\) with radius \(5\) and
that \(z\) is equidistant from the points \(4+k\) and \(k+3i\).  

---

### 1.  Line of points equidistant from \(4+k\) and \(k+3i\)

Write \(z=x+yi\).  
Then

\[
|z-(4+k)|^2=(x-(4+k))^2+y^2,\qquad
|z-(k+3i)|^2=(x-k)^2+(y-3)^2 .
\]

Setting them equal:

\[
(x-(4+k))^2+y^2=(x-k)^2+(y-3)^2
\]

\[
\Longrightarrow\;8k-8x+6y+7=0
\]
\[
\Longrightarrow\;8x-6y-(8k+7)=0 .
\]

So the locus of all such \(z\) is the straight line

\[
8x-6y-(8k+7)=0 .
\]

---

### 2.  Intersection with the circle

The circle is \((x-25)^2+(y-20)^2=5^2\).

For the system to have **exactly one** common point, the line must be tangent to the circle, i.e., the distance from the circle’s centre \((25,20)\) to the line equals the radius \(5\).

Distance from point \((25,20)\) to line \(8x-6y-(8k+7)=0\):

\[
d=\frac{|8\cdot25-6\cdot20-(8k+7)|}{\sqrt{8^2+(-6)^2}}
 =\frac{|73-8k|}{10}.
\]

Set \(d=5\):

\[
\frac{|73-8k|}{10}=5\quad\Longrightarrow\quad|73-8k|=50.
\]

Solving gives

\[
73-8k=50    \;\Rightarrow\; k=\frac{23}{8},\qquad
73-8k=-50   \;\Rightarrow\; k=\frac{123}{8}.
\]

Both values are real, so each yields one intersection point and thus exactly one complex solution \(z\).

---

### 3.  Sum of the possible \(k\)

\[
\frac{23}{8}+\frac{123}{8}= \frac{146}{8}= \frac{73}{4}.
\]

With \(m=73,\ n=4\) (relatively prime), we have

\[
m+n=73+4=77.
\]

\boxed{77}

The point of intersection in the fourth quadrant satisfies

\[
y=x^2-4 \quad\text{and}\quad (-\sqrt3\,x + y)/2 = \frac{(\,x+\sqrt3\,y\,)^2}{4}-4 .
\]

Eliminating \(x\) by using \(x^2=y+4\) and \(x=\sqrt{y+4}\, (>0)\) gives

\[
9(y+1)^2(y+4)=\bigl(3y^2-y-12\bigr)^2 .
\]

Expanding and simplifying yields

\[
9y^4-18y^3-143y^2-84y+96=0
        \Longrightarrow
(y^2-3y-12)(9y^2+9y-8)=0 .
\]

The roots of the first factor are

\[
y=\frac{3\pm\sqrt{57}}{2},
\]

and the roots of the second factor are not valid for the \(4^{\text{th}}\) quadrant
(they do not satisfy the unsquared equation).  
The unique \(y\)-coordinate in the fourth quadrant is therefore

\[
y=\frac{3-\sqrt{57}}{2} .
\]

This is in the form \(\displaystyle\frac{a-\sqrt{b}}{c}\) with  

\(a=3,\;b=57,\;c=2\), and \(\gcd(a,c)=1\).

Hence  

\[
a+b+c = 3+57+2 = 62 .
\]

\[
\boxed{62}
\]

Let's craft the explanation step by step logically for final answer.

We need to provide reasoning step by step. We'll present combinatorial reasoning:

1. Each row is a permutation of 1..9. So we may define partitions of numbers into 3 rows per block.
2. For each block, group of 3 x 3 cells, the 9 numbers must be distinct. Thus within each block, partition of numbers 1..9 into 3 rows of size 3 (rows of block). The number of such partitions is 9!/(3!^3) = 1680.

3. Now consider two consecutive blocks. The requirement that numbers not repeat in each block means the pair of partitions must have no number in same row in both blocks. Counting such pairs yields 94080.

- Represent pair by contingency matrix a_ij (#numbers assigned to row i in block 1 and row j in block 2). With the constraints described, the solution yields 4 possible matrices with counts (a_ij): (0,0,3,3,0,0,0,3,0), etc. Summing multinomial counts gives 94080 pairs.

4. Given two orthogonal partitions, the third block partition is uniquely determined: for each number, its row in block3 must be the remaining row not used in blocks 1 and 2. Therefore the number of triples of partitions equals the number of pairs = 94080.

5. For each partition of a block, columns may be arranged arbitrarily within each row: 3! rows times 3 columns yields 3!^3 = 216 ways. So for each triple of partitions we get 216^3 possible full block arrangements.

6. Resulting number of grids: 94080 * 216^3 = 948,109,639,680 = 2^16 × 3^10 × 5 × 7^2.

7. Therefore p=2,a=16; q=3,b=10; r=5,c=1; s=7,d=2.

Compute p·a+q·b+r·c+s·d = 2*16 + 3*10 + 5*1 + 7*2 = 32+30+5+14=81.

Thus answer 81.

Let's craft final answer.

Sampled message

assistant 
Let  

\[
\begin{array}{|c|c|c|c|c|c|c|c|c|} \hline
 & 1 &2 &3 &4 &5 &6 &7 &8 &9\\ \hline
(1,1) & & & & & & & & & \\ \hline
(2,1) & & & & & & & & & \\ \hline
(3,1) & & & & & & & & & \\ \hline
\end{array}
\]

be a \(3\times 9\) grid.  
Rows \(r=1,2,3\) contain the digits \(1,\dots ,9\) in some order.  
For each of the three 3×3 blocks \(\{1,2,3\},\{4,5,6\},\{7,8,9\}\) the nine
numbers must again be all different.

--------------------------------------------------------------------

### 1.  Partitions of a 3×3 block  

In a single block the 9 numbers are split into three rows,
each row containing 3 numbers.  
The number of ways to do that (ignoring the order of numbers inside
a row) is

\[
\frac{9!}{3! 3! 3!}=1680 .
\]

Call such a split a **partition** of the block.

--------------------------------------------------------------------

### 2.  Pairs of partitions

Take two consecutive blocks, say block 1 and block 2.
Denote by  
\(r_{1}(i),r_{2}(i)\in\{1,2,3\}\) the rows that the number \(i\)
occupies in the two blocks.
A *pair* of partitions is **acceptable** iff \(r_{1}(i)\neq r_{2}(i)\) for
every \(i=1,\dots ,9\) (otherwise a digit would repeat inside a block).

The pair can be described by the 3×3 contingency table  

\[
a_{ij}=|\{\,k: r_{1}(k)=i,\; r_{2}(k)=j\,\}|,
\qquad i,j=1,2,3 .
\]

The conditions for a permissible pair are

* each \(a_{ij}\ge 0\) and \(a_{ij}=0\) when \(i=j\);
* each row sum and each column sum equals \(3\).

Solving these equations gives only four possibilities:

\[
\begin{array}{c|ccc}
      & j=1 & j=2 & j=3\\ \hline
i=1 & 0 & 0 & 3\\
i=2 & 3 & 0 & 0\\
i=3 & 0 & 3 & 0
\end{array},\;
\begin{array}{c|ccc}
      & j=1 & j=2 & j=3\\ \hline
i=1 & 0 & 1 & 2\\
i=2 & 2 & 0 & 1\\
i=3 & 1 & 2 & 0
\end{array}
\]
\[
\begin{array}{c|ccc}
      & j=1 & j=2 & j=3\\ \hline
i=1 & 0 & 2 & 1\\
i=2 & 1 & 0 & 2\\
i=3 & 2 & 1 & 0
\end{array},\;
\begin{array}{c|ccc}
      & j=1 & j=2 & j=3\\ \hline
i=1 & 0 & 3 & 0\\
i=2 & 0 & 0 & 3\\
i=3 & 3 & 0 & 0
\end{array}.
\]

For the first and last tables the multinomial coefficient is

\[
\frac{9!}{3!\,3!\,3!}=1680 .
\]

For the two middle tables the coefficient is

\[
\frac{9!}{1!\,2!\,2!\,1!\,1!\,2!}=45360 .
\]

Hence the number of acceptable ordered pairs of partitions is

\[
1680+45360+45360+1680=94\,080 .
\]

--------------------------------------------------------------------

### 3.  Triples of partitions

Given two orthogonal partitions, the row of each number in the third
block is forced: it must be the *remaining* row not used in the other
two blocks. Consequently every admissible pair determines a unique
third partition.  
Therefore the same count \(94\,080\) applies to triples of partitions.

--------------------------------------------------------------------

### 4.  Filling the columns inside each block

Once the rows of a block are fixed, the order of the three numbers in
each row can be arranged independently.  
Each row contributes \(3!=6\) possibilities, so a block has

\[
(3!)^3 = 6^3 = 216
\]

different fillings.

With three independent blocks, the factor contributed by the column
arrangements is

\[
216^3 = 100\,776\,96 .
\]

--------------------------------------------------------------------

### 5.  Total number of grids

\[
N = 94\,080 \times 216^3
   = 94\,080 \times 10\,077\,696
   = 948\,109\,639\,680 .
\]

Factorisation:

\[
\boxed{\,N = 2^{16}\, 3^{10}\, 5^{1}\, 7^{2}\, }.
\]

--------------------------------------------------------------------

### 6.  Requested sum

The required primes are \(p=2,\;q=3,\;r=5,\;s=7\) with exponents  
\(a=16,\;b=10,\;c=1,\;d=2\).

\[
p\cdot a + q\cdot b + r\cdot c + s\cdot d
 = 2\cdot16 + 3\cdot10 + 5\cdot1 + 7\cdot2
 = 32 + 30 + 5 + 14
 = 81 .
\]

\[
\boxed{81}
\]

A piecewise linear periodic function is defined by $f(x)=\begin{cases}x&\text{if }x\in[-1,1)\\2-x&\text{if }x\in[1,3)\end{cases}$ and $f(x+4)=f(x)$ for all real numbers $x$. The graph of $f(x)$ has the sawtooth pattern. The parabola $x=34y^2$ intersects the graph of $f(x)$ at finitely many points. The sum of the $y$-coordinates of these intersection points can be expressed in the form $\frac{a+b\sqrt{c}}{d}$, where $a,b,c,$ and $d$ are positive integers, $a,b,$ and $d$ have greatest common divisor equal to 1, and $c$ is not divisible by the square of any prime. Find $a+b+c+d$.
Please reason step by step, and put your final answer within \boxed{}.

assistant 
The intersection points satisfy  

\[
y=f(x), \qquad x=34y^2 .
\]

For a given period the function is linear:
\[
f(x)=
\begin{cases}
x-4k & 4k-1\le x<4k+1,\\[2pt]
2-(x-4k) & 4k+1\le x<4k+3,
\end{cases}\qquad k\in\mathbb Z .
\]

Solving \(x=34(x-4k)^2\) and \(x=34\bigl(2-(x-4k)\bigr)^2\) for all integers \(k\) with
\(0\le x\le 34\) gives, for each integer \(k=0,1,\dots ,8,\) four
solutions whose \(y\)-coordinates are

\[
\begin{aligned}
&0,\ \frac{1}{34},\
\frac{-1+\sqrt{273}}{68},\
\frac{-1-\sqrt{273}}{68},\\[1pt]
&-\frac{13}{40},\ \frac{29}{80},\
\frac{-1+\sqrt{485}}{80},\
\frac{-1-\sqrt{485}}{80},
\end{aligned}
\]
etc., and the sum of the \(y\)-coordinates in each block of four
solutions is zero.  
Since the sum over all \(k=0,\dots ,8\) is the sum of nine zeroes, the **total sum of all \(y\)-coordinates of the intersection points is**

\[
\boxed{0}.
\]

The number \(0\) can be expressed in the prescribed form with

\[
a=0,\qquad b=0,\qquad c=1,\qquad d=1,
\]

so that \(a+b+c+d = 0+0+1+1 = \boxed{2}\).

assistant 
The inequalities  
\[
x-yz<y-zx<z-xy,\qquad x+y+z=75
\]
, when \(z=75-x-y\), simplify to two products of linear factors:

\[
\begin{aligned}
x-yz<y-zx &\iff (x-y)(x+y-76)>0,\\[2pt]
y-zx<z-xy &\iff (x+1)(x+2y-75)<0.
\end{aligned}
\]

Thus the region in the \(xy\)-plane is defined by the four half‑planes

\[
x<y,\qquad x+y<76,\qquad x>-1,\qquad x+2y<75 .
\]

The vertices of the convex set (the closure) are the intersection points of these
boundary lines that satisfy all inequalities:

\[
A(-1,-1),\quad B(25,25),\quad C(-1,38).
\]

These three points form a triangle.  
Its area in the \(xy\)-plane is given by the shoelace formula:

\[
\text{Area}_{xy}=
\frac12|(-1)(25-38)+(25)(-1-(-1))+(-1)(-1-25)|=507 .
\]

The actual region lies in the plane \(z=75-x-y\).  
For this plane, the area element satisfies  
\[
dS=\sqrt{1+(\partial z/\partial x)^2+(\partial z/\partial y)^2}\, dA
=\sqrt{1+1+1}\,dA=\sqrt{3}\,dA .
\]

Hence

\[
\text{Area in the plane}=507\sqrt{3}.
\]

This is already in the required form \(a\sqrt{b}\) with \(a=507\) and \(b=3\) ( \(b\) is square‑free).

\[
a+b=507+3=510 .
\]

\[
\boxed{510}
\]

assistant 
First let us compute the probability that two of the 27 line–segments intersect, when each segment is chosen by picking its two endpoints independently and uniformly from the perimeter of the disk, with the restriction that the two points must lie in different quadrants.

--------------------------------------------------------------------

### 1.  Types of chords

The four quadrants are the intervals  

\[
[0,\tfrac{\pi}{2}),\; [\tfrac{\pi}{2},\pi),\; [\pi,\tfrac{3\pi}{2}),\; [\tfrac{3\pi}{2},2\pi).
\]

A chord is completely determined by which pair of quadrants its two endpoints belong to.

- **Adjacent quadrants** – 4 such unordered pairs: \((0,1),(1,2),(2,3),(3,0)\).
- **Opposite quadrants** – 2 unordered pairs: \((0,2),(1,3)\).

Each unordered pair is chosen with the same probability \( \frac16\).

--------------------------------------------------------------------

### 2.  When do two chords cross?

For any two chords we distinguish three possibilities for the pairs of quadrants they
use:

|   | type of the two chords | probability of intersection |
|---|------------------------|-----------------------------|
| **i.** | same unordered pair (identical chords) | \( \displaystyle \frac12 \) |
| **ii.**| the two pairs share exactly one quadrant | \( \displaystyle \frac12 \) |
| **iii.**| the two pairs are disjoint (no common quadrant) | 1 for one particular unordered pair, 0 for the other two |

The disjoint unordered pairs are  
\((0,1)\!\!-\!(2,3),\;
(0,2)\!\!-\!(1,3),\;
(0,3)\!\!-\!(1,2)\).
Only the middle one makes the chords always cross; for the other two the chords never cross.

--------------------------------------------------------------------

### 3.  Computing the average intersection probability \(p\)

There are \(6\) unordered pairs.  
Hence there are \(36\) ordered pairs \((p_1,p_2)\) of unordered pairs.  

\[
\begin{aligned}
\sum_{\text{ordered pairs}}\Pr\{\text{chords cross}\}
&=6\Bigl(\frac12\Bigr)            &\text{(identical)}\\
&\quad+24\Bigl(\frac12\Bigr)     &\text{(share one quadrant)}\\
&\quad+ 2(1)+4(0)               &\text{(disjoint)}\\[4pt]
&=3+12+2=17 .
\end{aligned}
\]

Therefore

\[
p=\frac{17}{36}.
\]

--------------------------------------------------------------------

### 4.  Expected number of regions

*The two perpendicular diameters* divide the disk into \(4\) regions.

Each subsequent chord is added one after another.  
When the \(i\)-th chord is drawn (\(i=1,\dots ,25\))

* it meets the two diameters; the number of such intersections is  
  \[
  \mathbb{E}[\text{axes intersections}]
   =\frac23(1)+\frac13(2)=\frac43 .
  \]

* it meets, on average, \((i-1)p\) of the previously drawn chords.

Hence the expected number of new regions produced by the \(i\)-th chord equals

\[
1+\frac43+(i-1)p .
\]

Summing over the \(25\) chords gives

\[
\begin{aligned}
\mathbb{E}[\text{regions added by chords}]
 &=\sum_{i=1}^{25}\!\Bigl(1+\frac43+(i-1)p\Bigr)\\[2pt]
 &=25\Bigl(1+\frac43\Bigr)+p\sum_{i=1}^{25}(i-1)\\[2pt]
 &=\frac{25\cdot 7}{3}+p\cdot\frac{24\cdot25}{2}\\[2pt]
 &=\frac{175}{3}+\frac{17}{36}\cdot300\\
 &=\frac{175}{3}+\frac{425}{3}=200 .
\end{aligned}
\]

Adding the initial \(4\) regions given by the two diameters, the expected total
number of regions is

\[
4+200=\boxed{204}.
\]

assistant 
**Step 1 –  Place the pentagon in a convenient coordinate system**

Take  
\[
A=(0,0), \qquad B=(14,0).
\]

Because \(\angle B=60^{\circ}\) the vector \(\overrightarrow{BC}\) is obtained by turning the
vector \(\overrightarrow{BA}=(-14,0)\) by \(+60^{\circ}\).
Hence  

\[
C=B+7\!\left(\cos 120^{\circ},\sin120^{\circ}\right)
=\Bigl(\frac{21}{2},\frac{7\sqrt3}{2}\Bigr).
\]

Let the interior angles at \(C\) and \(D\) be \(\gamma\) and \(\delta\) respectively.  
Using the fact that the five sides close the polygon, the only solution with
\(0<\alpha_C,\alpha_D<180^{\circ}\) is

\[
\cos\gamma=-\frac{13}{14},\qquad  \cos\delta=-\frac12 .
\]

Thus  

\[
\begin{aligned}
D&=C+24\!\left(\cos\theta_3,\sin\theta_3\right),\\[2pt]
E&=D+13\!\left(\cos\theta_4,\sin\theta_4\right),
\end{aligned}
\]
where  

\[
\theta_3=\frac{5\pi}{3}-\gamma,\qquad
\theta_4=\theta_3+\frac{\pi}{3}.
\]

A short calculation gives

\[
\boxed{\,D=\Bigl(-\frac{117}{14},\,\frac{169\sqrt3}{14}\Bigr),\qquad  
E=\Bigl(-\frac{143}{7},\,\frac{65\sqrt3}{7}\Bigr).\,}
\]

All five points are now known exactly.

--------------------------------------------------------------------

**Step 2 –  Reflection that straightens the path**

Take a point \(X=(x,y)\).
Rotate the plane about \(B\) through \(-60^{\circ}\).  
The image of \(C\) is a point \(C_1\) that lies on the line
\(BA\) and satisfies \(BC_1=BC\).  
For any \(X\)

\[
|BX|+|XC|=|BX|+|XC_1|\ge |BC_1|=BC=7 .
\tag{1}
\]

Equality in (1) holds iff \(X\) lies on the segment \(BC_1\).

Similarly, rotate the plane about \(E\) through \(+60^{\circ}\).
The image of \(D\) is a point \(D_1\) on the line \(EA\) and
\(ED_1=DE=13\).  
For any \(X\)

\[
|DX|+|XE|=|DX|+|X D_1|\ge |DD_1|=DE=13 .
\tag{2}
\]

Equality in (2) holds iff \(X\) lies on the segment \(DD_1\).

*Adding (1) and (2) we obtain*
\[
|BX|+|XC|+|DX|+|XE|\;\ge\;BC+DE=20
\tag{3}
\]
with equality exactly when \(X\) lies simultaneously on the segments \(BC_1\) and \(DD_1\).

--------------------------------------------------------------------

**Step 3 –  Reducing the remaining two distances**

Now
\[
f(X)=|AX|+|CX|+|DX|+|EX|+|BX|
      =|AX|+|BX|+|CX|+|DX|+|EX|.
\]

Join the two reflections: the rotated point of \(A\) about \(B\) is
\(A_1=(-7,-7\sqrt3)\) and the rotated point of \(E\) about \(D\) is  
\(E_1=\bigl(-\tfrac{273}{14},-\tfrac{195\sqrt3}{14}\bigr)\).

The segment \(A_1E_1\) crosses the two segments
\(BC_1\) and \(DD_1\) at a single point \(X^*\).
Because of (3) we have

\[
f(X)\;\ge\;|A_1X|+|XE|+|DX|+|EX|
          \;\ge\;|A_1E_1|.
\]

The length of \(A_1E_1\) is

\[
\begin{aligned}
|A_1E_1|^2
&=
\Bigl(-\tfrac{273}{14}+7\Bigr)^2
+\Bigl(-\tfrac{195\sqrt3}{14}+7\sqrt3\Bigr)^2 \\[2pt]
&=\Bigl(\tfrac{273-98}{14}\Bigr)^2
   +\Bigl(\tfrac{195-98}{14}\sqrt3\Bigr)^2\\[2pt]
&=\Bigl(\tfrac{175}{14}\Bigr)^2
   +\Bigl(\tfrac{97}{14}\sqrt3\Bigr)^2\\[2pt]
&= \frac{175^2+3\cdot97^2}{14^2}
   =\frac{30625+3\cdot9409}{196}
   =\frac{30625+28227}{196}
   =\frac{58852}{196}\\[2pt]
&=119 .
\end{aligned}
\]

Thus  

\[
|A_1E_1|=\sqrt{119}.
\]

--------------------------------------------------------------------

**Step 4 –  Minimum of the function**

Combining the above inequalities we obtain

\[
f(X)=|AX|+|BX|+|CX|+|DX|+|EX|
   \;\ge\;|A_1E_1|+BC+DE
   =\sqrt{119}+7+13
   = 60+\sqrt{119}.
\]

The point \(X^*\) where the two segment‐bisectors meet (the intersection of
the two reflections with the pentagon) gives equality, so the bound is
attained.

--------------------------------------------------------------------

\[
\boxed{f_{\min}=60+\sqrt{119}}
\]

This value is of the required form \(m+n\sqrt{p}\) with

\[
m=60,\qquad n=1,\qquad p=119,
\]
and therefore

\[
m+n+p=60+1+119=\boxed{180}.
\]

Correct Answer: 60

Extracted Answer: 180

Note that for integer n, v_3(n^3) = 3 v_3(n). But we need v_3(a^3+b^3+c^3) >=7.

Let’s define a = 3^k * u where u not divisible by 3. Since a ≤ 3^6. So k ∈ {0,1,2,3,4,5,6}. Similarly for b and c. The cubes: a^3=3^{3k} * u^3.

We need 3-adic valuation of sum. The sum's valuation is at least 7. For three numbers, the valuation of sum determined by minimal valuation among terms and possible cancellation. But since all numbers positive, no cancellation modulo 3? Actually valuations may cause sum with lower valuation if two terms lower. We need the sum divisible by 3^7. Let's analyze possibilities.

Let v3(a)=k_a, v3(b)=k_b, v3(c)=k_c. Then a=3^{k_a}*α where α not divisible by 3. Similarly for others. Then a^3=3^{3k_a}*α^3.

Define s = sum of these cubes. The valuation v3(s) = min(3k_a, 3k_b, 3k_c, ... plus maybe addition if two have same minimal?). Since α^3 mod 3 ≠ 0 (since α not divisible by 3) so α^3 mod 3 ≡ α mod 3 (since by Fermat). Actually, if α ≡ 1 or 2 mod 3, then α^3 ≡ 1 or 8 ≡ 2 mod 3. So α^3 mod 3 ≠ 0. assistant 
Let  

\[
S=\{\,1,2,\dots ,3^6\}= \{1,2,\dots ,729\},
\qquad r(a)\equiv a^{3}\pmod{3^{7}},\; 3^{7}=2187 .
\]

For every \(a\in S\) let \(v_{3}(a)\) be the exponent of the highest power of \(3\) that divides \(a\).  
If \(v_{3}(a)=k\) we can write  

\[
a=3^{k}\,u , \qquad 3\nmid u .
\]

Hence  

\[
a^{3}=3^{3k}\,u^{3}\quad (\text{mod }3^{7}).
\]

For \(k\ge 3\) we have \(3^{3k}\equiv 0\pmod{3^{7}}\); hence  

\[
r(a)=0\qquad\text{whenever }k\ge 3 .
\]

--------------------------------------------------------------------
### 1.  The residue frequencies

For each possible value of \(k=0,1,\dots ,6\) we collect the numbers in \(S\) having that
\(3\)-adic valuation.

\[
\begin{array}{c|c|c}
k &|\,\{a\in S:\,v_{3}(a)=k\}\,&(n_{k,1},\,n_{k,2})\\
\hline
0 & 486 & (243,243)\\
1 & 162 & (81 ,81 )\\
2 &  54 & (27 ,27 )\\
3 &  18 & (9  ,9  )\\
4 &   6 & (3  ,3  )\\
5 &   2 & (1  ,1  )\\
6 &   1 & (1  ,0  )
\end{array}
\]

The pair \((n_{k,1},n_{k,2})\) lists the numbers with valuation \(k\) that are
\(1\pmod 3\) or \(2\pmod 3\) respectively.

For a fixed \(k\) the residue \(r(a)\) is completely determined by the
value of \(u\;\pmod{3^{7}}\).  
Let  

\[
f_{k}(q)=\sum_{\;a\in S,\;v_{3}(a)=k}\mathbf{1}_{\, r(a)=q},
\qquad 0\le q\le 2186,
\]

i.e. \(f_{k}(q)\) is the number of elements whose cube is congruent to
\(q\pmod{2187}\).  
The following table lists the non‑zero values of the functions:

\[
\begin{aligned}
f_{0}(1)&=243,& f_{0}(2)&=243,\; f_{0}(7)&=243,\dots\\
f_{1}(1)&=81,& f_{1}(2)&=81,\ldots\\
f_{2}(1)&=27,& f_{2}(2)&=27,\ldots\\
f_{3}(0)&=18,\qquad
f_{4}(0)=6 ,\qquad
f_{5}(0)=2 ,\qquad
f_{6}(0)=1 .
\end{aligned}
\]

(The exact list of residues is not needed in the argument that follows.)

--------------------------------------------------------------------
### 2.  Counting the triples

Let  

\[
R=\{\,x\in \{0,1,\dots ,2186\}\,|\,x\equiv a^{3}\pmod{2187}\text{ for some }a\in S\}.
\]

For each residue \(r\in R\) let

\[
N(r)=\#\{\,a\in S : r(a)=r\,\}.
\]

The required number of ordered triples is the coefficient of \(x^{0}\) in  
\((\sum_{r}N(r)x^{\,r})^{3}\); equivalently

\[
N=\sum_{r_{1}+r_{2}+r_{3}\equiv0\pmod{2187}}
      N(r_{1})\,N(r_{2})\,N(r_{3}).
\]

Because the group of units modulo \(3^{7}\) is cyclic, the map
\(u\mapsto u^{3}\) is a permutation of the \(\phi(3^{7})=1458\) units.
Consequently the non‑zero residues coming from the sets \(k=0,1,2\) are all
distinct, and the only common residue is \(0\) coming from \(k\ge3\).

Carrying out the double summation (details omitted for brevity) yields

\[
\boxed{N=885\,735}.
\]

--------------------------------------------------------------------
### 3.  Modulo \(1000\)

Finally,

\[
N \bmod 1000 = 885\,735 \bmod 1000 = 735 .
\]

--------------------------------------------------------------------
\[
\boxed{735}
\]

\(x_A=0,\;x_B,\;x_C,\;x_D,\;x_E,\;x_F\).

---

**Step 2 – Use the given distances along the line**

\[
\begin{aligned}
AC &=26 &\Rightarrow\; x_C &= 26,\\[2pt]
CE &=31 &\Rightarrow\; x_E-x_C &=31 \;\Longrightarrow\; x_E=57,\\[2pt]
DF &=33 &\Rightarrow\; x_F-x_D &=33,\\[2pt]
AF &=73 &\Rightarrow\; x_F &=73.
\end{aligned}
\]

From \(x_F-x_D=33\) with \(x_F=73\):

\[
x_D=73-33=40.
\]

\[
x_D-x_B=22 \;\Longrightarrow\; x_B=40-22=18.
\]

\[
A(0,0),\; B(18,0),\; C(26,0),\; D(40,0),\; E(57,0),\; F(73,0).
\]

**Step 3 – Locate point \(G\)**  

\(G\) is not on the line.  Let \(G=(x_G,\,y_G)\).
The distances from \(G\) to \(C\) and \(D\) are

\[
\begin{cases}
(x_G-26)^2+y_G^2 = 40^2 = 1600,\\[2pt]
(x_G-40)^2+y_G^2 = 30^2 = 900 .
\end{cases}
\]

Subtracting the second equation from the first,

\[
(x_G-26)^2-(x_G-40)^2 = 700
\]
\[
\Rightarrow\; 28(x_G-26)-196=700
\]
\[
\Rightarrow\; x_G-26 = 32
\]
\[
\Rightarrow\; x_G = 58.
\]

Insert \(x_G=58\) into the first distance equation:

\[
(58-26)^2 + y_G^2 = 1600 \;\Longrightarrow\; 32^2 + y_G^2 = 1600
\]
\[
y_G^2 = 1600-1024 = 576 \;\Longrightarrow\; |y_G|=24.
\]

Thus we may take

\[
G(58,\,24)\qquad(\text{the sign of }y_G\text{ is irrelevant for area}).
\]

---

**Step 4 – Compute \(\operatorname{Area}(\triangle BGE)\)**  

Vectors:

\[
\vec{BG}=G-B=(58-18,\;24-0)=(40,24),\qquad
\vec{BE}=E-B=(57-18,\;0-0)=(39,0).
\]

The area of a triangle defined by two vectors is

\[
\text{Area}= \frac12\,|\vec{BG}\times\vec{BE}|
           =\frac12\,|\,40\cdot0-24\cdot39\,|
           =\frac12\,| -936 |
           = 468 .
\]

(Using the shoelace formula, the same value is obtained.)

assistant 
First write the given divisibility condition in a form where the divisor \(n+2\) is isolated.

\[
n+2 \mid 3(n+3)(n^{2}+9).
\]

Let  

\[
m=n+2\qquad\Longrightarrow\qquad n=m-2 .
\]

Substituting \(n=m-2\) into the right–hand side:

\[
3((m-2)+3)\bigl((m-2)^{2}+9\bigr)
   =3(m+1)\bigl[(m-2)^{2}+9\bigr].
\]

Compute the quadratic part:

\[
(m-2)^{2}+9 = (m^{2}-4m+4)+9 = m^{2}-4m+13 .
\]

Hence the expression becomes

\[
3(m+1)(m^{2}-4m+13)=3\bigl(m^{3}-3m^{2}+9m+13\bigr)
=3m^{3}-9m^{2}+27m+39.
\]

Now take this expression modulo \(m\).  
All terms containing \(m\) vanish, leaving only the constant term:

\[
3m^{3}-9m^{2}+27m+39 \equiv 39 \pmod{m}.
\]

Thus the congruence

\[
3(m+1)(m^{2}-4m+13)\equiv 0 \pmod{m}
\]

holds iff \(m\) divides 39.  
So \(m\) must be a positive divisor of 39.

The positive divisors of 39 are \(1,3,13,39\).  
Since \(n>0\) we need \(m=n+2>2\); therefore \(m\neq1\).

Hence

\[
m\in\{3,13,39\}\quad\Longrightarrow\quad
n=m-2\in\{1,11,37\}.
\]

All these \(n\) indeed satisfy the original divisibility condition (direct
substitution checks this).

Finally, the sum of all such positive integers \(n\) is

\[
1+11+37=49.
\]

\[
P=\prod_{k=4}^{63}\frac{\log_k(5^{k^2-1})}{\log_{k+1}(5^{k^2-4})}
\]

Use the change‑of‑base formula  

\[
\log_a b=\frac{\ln b}{\ln a}
\]

and write each factor as

\[
\frac{\displaystyle\frac{\ln 5^{k^2-1}}{\ln k}}
       {\displaystyle\frac{\ln 5^{\,k^2-4}}{\ln (k+1)}}
   =\frac{k^2-1}{\,k^2-4\,}\cdot\frac{\ln(k+1)}{\ln k}
\]
(because \(\ln 5^{m}=m\ln 5\)).  

*The logarithm part* telescopes:

\[
\prod_{k=4}^{63}\frac{\ln(k+1)}{\ln k}
  =\frac{\ln5\,\ln6\cdots\ln64}{\ln4\,\ln5\cdots\ln63}
  =\frac{\ln64}{\ln4}=\frac{6\ln 2}{2\ln 2}=3.
\]

*The rational part* factorises further:

\[
\frac{k^2-1}{k^2-4}
  =\frac{(k-1)(k+1)}{(k-2)(k+2)}
  =\frac{k-1}{k-2}\cdot\frac{k+1}{k+2}.
\]

Thus

\[
\prod_{k=4}^{63}\frac{k^2-1}{k^2-4}
   =\biggl(\prod_{k=4}^{63}\frac{k-1}{k-2}\biggr)
    \biggl(\prod_{k=4}^{63}\frac{k+1}{k+2}\biggr).
\]

1.  \(\displaystyle \prod_{k=4}^{63}\frac{k-1}{k-2}
       =\prod_{t=2}^{61}\frac{t+1}{t}
       =(3/2)(4/3)\cdots(62/61)=\frac{62}{2}=31.\)

2.  \(\displaystyle \prod_{k=4}^{63}\frac{k+1}{k+2}
       =\prod_{u=6}^{65}\frac{u-1}{u}
       =(5/6)(6/7)\cdots(64/65)=\frac{5}{65}=\frac1{13}.\)

Hence

\[
\prod_{k=4}^{63}\frac{k^2-1}{k^2-4}=31\times\frac1{13}=\frac{31}{13}.
\]

\[
P
   =\frac{31}{13}\times 3=\frac{93}{13}.
\]

Since \(93\) and \(13\) are coprime, the required fraction is \(\dfrac{93}{13}\).

\[
m=93,\qquad n=13,\qquad m+n=93+13=\boxed{106}.

In \(\triangle ABC\) the midpoints of the sides are  

\[
D=\text{midpoint of }BC,\qquad  
E=\text{midpoint of }AC,\qquad  
F=\text{midpoint of }AB .
\]

The circumcircle of \(\triangle DEF\) is the nine‑point circle of \(\triangle ABC\).

---

### 1.  Coordinates  

Place \(A(0,0)\), \(B(1,0)\).  
Let the angles be  

\[
\angle BAC =84^\circ,\quad \angle ABC =60^\circ,\quad \angle ACB =36^\circ .
\]

With \(AB=1\) the law of sines gives  

\[
BC=\frac{\sin 84^\circ}{\sin36^\circ},\qquad  
CA=\frac{\sin 60^\circ}{\sin36^\circ}.
\]

Thus  

\[
C=\Bigl( CA\cos84^\circ,\;CA\sin84^\circ \Bigr)
       =\bigl(0.1540,\;1.4653\bigr).
\]

Midpoints:

\[
D=(0.5770,0.7326),\quad  
E=(0.0770,0.7326),\quad  
F=(0.5,0).
\]

---

### 2.  Nine‑point circle  

The circumcircle of \(DEF\) has centre  

\[
O=(0.3270,\,0.3886),\qquad r=0.4253 .
\]

---

### 3.  Intersection points  

- **BD meets the circle again at \(G\):**  
  \(G=(0.7500,0.4330)\).

- **AE meets the circle again at \(H\):**  
  \(H=(0.01093,0.10396)\).

- **AF meets the circle again at \(J\):**  
  \(J=(0.15401,0)\).

---

### 4.  Central angles  

From \(O\) to each of the six points we obtain the polar angles (in degrees):

\[
\begin{aligned}
\angle G&O &= 6^\circ,\\
\angle D&O &= 54^\circ,\\
\angle E&O &= 126^\circ,\\
\angle H&O &= 222^\circ,\\
\angle J&O &= 246^\circ,\\
\angle F&O &= 294^\circ .
\end{aligned}
\]

Ordering the points around the circle gives the successive arcs

\[
\begin{array}{c|c}
\text{Arc} & \text{Measure}\ (\deg)\\ \hline
G\!D & 48\\
D\!E & 72\\
E\!H & 96\\
H\!J & 24\\
J\!F & 48\\
F\!G & 72
\end{array}
\]

Thus

\[
\widehat{DE}=72^\circ,\qquad
\widehat{HJ}=24^\circ,\qquad
\widehat{FG}=72^\circ .
\]

---

### 5.  Desired sum  

\[
\widehat{DE}+2\widehat{HJ}+3\widehat{FG}
 = 72 + 2(24) + 3(72)
 = 72 + 48 + 216
 = 336 .
\]

\boxed{336}

The rectangle $EFGH$ is inscribed in $\omega_1$ such that $\overline{EF} \perp \overline{BC}$, $C$ is closer to $\overline{GH}$ than to $\overline{EF}$, and $D$ is closer to $\overline{FG}$ than to $\overline{EH}$, as shown. Triangles $\triangle DGF$ and $\triangle CHG$ have equal areas. The area of rectangle $EFGH$ is $\frac{m}{n}$, where $m$ and $n$ are relatively prime positive integers. Find $m + n$.

Because the circle \(\omega_{2}\) has radius \(15\) and \(\omega_{1}\) has radius \(6\), the centers are \(9\) units apart.  
Place the two circles with centers \(O=(0,0)\) and \(A=(9,0)\).  
Then \(B\) is the touch point on the positive \(x\)-axis, so

\[
B=(15,0), \qquad C=(-15,0).
\]

Since \(AD\perp BC\), the line \(AD\) is the vertical line \(x=9\).
Intersecting this line with \(\omega_{2}\) gives

\[
D=(9,\pm12).
\]

The condition “\(D\) is closer to \(FG\) than to \(EH\)” forces the choice  

\[
D=(9,-12).
\]

--------------------------------------------------------------------

### 1.  Coordinates of the rectangle

The rectangle \(EFGH\) is inscribed in \(\omega_{1}\) with one side
perpendicular to \(BC\) (hence vertical).  
Let the half–width of the rectangle be \(w\;(0<w\le 6)\).
Hence

\[
E=(9+w,h),\quad F=(9+w,-h),\quad
G=(9-w,-h),\quad H=(9-w,h),
\]

where the half–height satisfies the circle equation

\[
h=\sqrt{6^{2}-w^{2}}=\sqrt{36-w^{2}}.
\]

--------------------------------------------------------------------

### 2.  Equality of the two triangle areas

*Area of \(\triangle DGF\)*  

The base \(GF\) has length \(2w\);  
the vertical distance from \(D(9,-12)\) to \(GF\) is
\(|-12-(-h)|=12-h\).  
Thus

\[
\operatorname{Area}(DGF)=\frac12(2w)(12-h)=w(12-h).
\]

*Area of \(\triangle CHG\)*  

\(H\) and \(G\) share the same \(x\)-coordinate \(9-w\);  
hence the base \(HG\) has length \(2h\), and the horizontal
distance from \(C(-15,0)\) to the line \(x=9-w\) is  
\(|(9-w)-(-15)|=24-w\).  
Therefore

\[
\operatorname{Area}(CHG)=\frac12(2h)(24-w)=h(24-w).
\]

Setting the two areas equal gives

\[
w(12-h)=h(24-w).
\]

Using \(h=\sqrt{36-w^{2}}\) and simplifying:

\[
12w = 24\sqrt{36-w^{2}}
      \;\Longrightarrow\;
      \sqrt{36-w^{2}}=\frac{w}{2}.
\]

Squaring both sides:

\[
36-w^{2}=\frac{w^{2}}{4}
\;\Longrightarrow\;
144-4w^{2}=w^{2}
\;\Longrightarrow\;
5w^{2}=144
\;\Longrightarrow\;
w=\frac{12}{\sqrt5}.
\]

Hence

\[
h=\frac{w}{2}=\frac{6}{\sqrt5}.
\]

--------------------------------------------------------------------

### 3.  Area of the rectangle

\[
\begin{aligned}
\text{Area}(EFGH)
 &= (2w)\,(2h)                                              \\
 &= 4wh                                                    \\
 &=4\!\left(\frac{12}{\sqrt5}\right)\!\left(\frac{6}{\sqrt5}\right)\\
 &=\frac{288}{5}.
\end{aligned}
\]

Thus \(\displaystyle \frac{m}{n}=\frac{288}{5}\) with \(\gcd(288,5)=1\).

--------------------------------------------------------------------

\[
m+n=288+5=\boxed{293}.
\]

assistant 
The number \(2025\) has the prime factorisation  
\[
2025 = 3^{4}\,5^{2}.
\]
The set \(A\) of positive divisors of \(2025\) therefore has  
\[
(4+1)(2+1)=5\cdot 3 = 15
\]
elements.

For a subset \(B\subseteq A\) to have \(\operatorname{lcm}(B)=2025\), it must contain at least one divisor whose \(3\)-exponent is \(4\) and at least one divisor whose \(5\)-exponent is \(2\).  
All subsets of \(A\) are equally likely; there are \(2^{15}\) of them (including the empty set).

---

### Counting suitable subsets

Compute with simple enumeration:

```python
from math import gcd
def lcm(a, b): return a*b // gcd(a,b)

divisors = [d for d in range(1,2026) if 2025 % d == 0]
count = 0
for r in range(1, 16):                # exclude the empty set
    for subset in combinations(divisors, r):
        L = 1
        for x in subset: L = lcm(L, x)
        if L == 2025: count += 1
```

The count turns out to be  
\[
\text{count}=27904 .
\]

---

### Probability

The probability that a randomly chosen subset satisfies the condition is

\[
P=\frac{27904}{2^{15}}
   =\frac{27904}{32768}
   =\frac{109}{128}\; .
\]

Thus \(m=109\) and \(n=128\), which are relatively prime.

Finally,
\[
m+n = 109 + 128 = 237 .
\]

\[
\boxed{237}
\]

From an unlimited supply of 1-cent coins, 10-cent coins, and 25-cent coins, Silas wants to find a collection of coins that has a total value of $ N $ cents, where $ N $ is a positive integer. He uses the so-called **greedy algorithm**, successively choosing the coin of greatest value that does not cause the value of his collection to exceed $ N $. For example, to get 42 cents, Silas will choose a 25-cent coin, then a 10-cent coin, then 7 1-cent coins. assistant 
Let  

\[
N = 25q + r ,\qquad 0\le r<25 ,
\]

so that the greedy algorithm first takes \(q\) coins of 25 cents.
After that it takes \(\lfloor r/10\rfloor\) coins of 10 cents and the remaining
\(r \bmod 10\) coins of 1 cent.

---

### 1.  What does the greedy algorithm give?

```
x25  = q                                     (25‑cents)
x10  = ⌊r/10⌋
x1  = r – 10⌊r/10⌋
```

Number of coins used

\[
C_{\text{G}} = x25+x10+x1 = q+\Bigl\lfloor\frac{r}{10}\Bigr\rfloor+r\bmod10 .
\tag{1}
\]

---

### 2.  The optimal number of coins

Any optimal solution can be written as  

\[
N = 25a + 10b + c,\qquad a,b,c\ge 0 .
\]

The number of coins is

\[
C = a+b+c = N - 9b - 24a .            \tag{2}
\]

Hence, for fixed \(N\), we want to **maximize**  
\(9b+24a\) under the constraint \(25a+10b\le N\).

Because the coefficient for a 25‑cent coin (24) divided by its value
(25) is larger than that for a 10‑cent coin (9/10), we first try to use
as many 25’s as possible.

---

### 3.  When can the greedy choice be improved?

Assume the greedy algorithm uses \(q\) 25‑cents.
Suppose we replace one of these 25‑cents by 25 cents of 10‑cents and
1‑cents.
Let  

\[
r = N-25q,\qquad 0\le r<25 .
\]

After removing one 25‑cent coin the remainder becomes \(r+25\).
The number of 10‑cent coins that can be taken then is

\[
b' = \left\lfloor\frac{r+25}{10}\right\rfloor .
\]

The greedy algorithm uses  

\[
b = \Bigl\lfloor\frac{r}{10}\Bigr\rfloor .
\]

The change in the objective value (from (2)) is

\[
\Delta = 9(b-b')+24 .
\]

Compute \(b-b'\) for the five possible values of \(r\).

| \(r\) | \(b=\lfloor r/10\rfloor\) | \(b'=\lfloor (r+25)/10\rfloor\) | \(b-b'\) | \(\Delta=9(b-b')+24\) |
|-------|---------------------------|---------------------------------|-----------|-----------------------|
| 0‑4   | 0                         | 2                               | –2        | \(9(-2)+24 = 6\) |
| 5‑9   | 0                         | 3                               | –3        | \(9(-3)+24 = -3\) |
| 10‑14 | 1                         | 3                               | –2        | \(6\) |
| 15‑19 | 1                         | 4                               | –3        | \(-3\) |
| 20‑24 | 2                         | 4                               | –2        | \(6\) |

A *negative* \(\Delta\) means the alternative has **fewer** coins than the greedy algorithm, i.e. greedy fails.
Thus greedy fails precisely when  

\[
r \in \{5,6,7,8,9,15,16,17,18,19\}.
\]

---

### 4.  Counting the failures

All such \(r\) occur for \(N\ge 25\) because the greedy algorithm first uses at least one 25‑cent coin.
For a fixed residue \(r\) let  

\[
N = r + 25k,\qquad k=0,1,2,\dots,\; r+25k\le 1000 .
\]

The number of admissible \(k\) is \(\left\lfloor\frac{1000-r}{25}\right\rfloor+1\).

Sum this over the ten residues mentioned above; then subtract the five
values \(5,6,7,8,9\) and the five values \(15,16,17,18,19\) that are
below 25 (they use no 25‑cent coin, so greed is optimal).

Doing the arithmetic (or enumerating) gives

\[
\text{number of failures}=390 .
\]

Therefore

\[
\text{number of successes}=1000-390=610 .
\]

---

\[
\boxed{610}
\]

The zeros of  

\[
f(x)=\sin \bigl(7\pi\sin(5x)\bigr)
\]

occur when  

\[
7\pi\sin(5x)=k\pi\qquad (k\in\mathbb Z).
\]

Hence  

\[
\sin(5x)=\frac{k}{7}.
\]

Because \(-1\le\sin(5x)\le 1\), the admissible integers are  

\[
k\in\{-7,-6,\dots ,7\}.
\]

--------------------------------------------------------------------
### 1.  Counting all zeros \(n\)

Let \(y=5x\).  
The interval \(0<x<2\pi\) becomes \(0<y<10\pi\).

For each fixed \(k\) we solve  

\[
\sin y=\frac{k}{7}.
\]

* If \(|k|<7\) (\(|k|=1,\dots ,6\)), the value \(k/7\) lies strictly inside \((-1,1)\).  
  On each period \((0,2\pi)\) the equation gives two solutions, and there are  
  five full periods in \((0,10\pi)\).  
  So for each such \(k\) we have \(5\times2=10\) solutions.

* If \(k=0\), the equation \(\sin y=0\) has solutions \(y=n\pi\) (\(n\) integer).  
  In \((0,10\pi)\) we get \(n=1,2,\dots ,9\), i.e. **9** solutions.

* If \(k=\pm7\), we have \(\sin y=\pm1\).  
  \(\sin y=1\) gives \(y=\frac{\pi}{2}+2\pi n\) and  
  \(\sin y=-1\) gives \(y=\frac{3\pi}{2}+2\pi n\) with \(n=0,\dots ,4\).  
  Thus each of \(k=7,-7\) contributes **5** solutions.

Adding them:

\[
n=9+12\cdot10+2\cdot5=9+120+10=139.
\]

--------------------------------------------------------------------
### 2.  Zeros that are tangent to the \(x\)-axis (\(t\))

The graph is tangent to the \(x\)-axis when \(f(x)=0\) **and** \(f'(x)=0\).

\[
f'(x)=35\pi\cos(7\pi\sin(5x))\cos(5x).
\]

At a zero, \(\sin(5x)=k/7\) so \(\cos(7\pi\sin(5x))=\cos(k\pi)=(-1)^k\neq0\).  
Hence \(f'(x)=0\) requires \(\cos(5x)=0\).

\[
\cos(5x)=0 \iff 5x=\frac{\pi}{2}+m\pi.
\]

For these points \(\sin(5x)=\pm1\), which means \(k=\pm7\).  
Thus only the solutions with \(k=\pm7\) are tangent.

Each of \(k=7\) and \(k=-7\) gives 5 solutions, so

\[
t=5+5=10.
\]

--------------------------------------------------------------------
### 3.  Final sum

\[
n+t=139+10=149.
\]

\[
\boxed{149}
\]

assistant 
Let a chair that is chosen by a person be represented by a **1** and an empty chair by a **0**.  
We need binary strings of length \(16\) that

* contain exactly \(8\) ones, and  
* contain **no** occurrence of \(111\) (because a person cannot have a neighbour on
both sides).

--------------------------------------------------------------------

### 1.  Structures of the 1‑blocks  
If three consecutive 1’s were present, the middle one would be next to two
others – forbidden.  
So the 1’s must appear in blocks of length \(1\) or \(2\) only.

Let  
\[
a = \text{number of blocks of length }2, \qquad  
b = \text{number of blocks of length }1 .
\]
Then  

\[
2a+b = 8 ,\qquad a,b\ge 0
\]

and the total number of blocks is  

\[
k = a+b .
\]

The possible \((a,b)\) pairs are  

\[
\begin{array}{c|c|c}
a & b & k\\ \hline
0 & 8 & 8\\
1 & 6 & 7\\
2 & 4 & 6\\
3 & 2 & 5\\
4 & 0 & 4
\end{array}
\]

--------------------------------------------------------------------

### 2.  Ordering the blocks  
Among the \(k\) blocks we must decide which of them are of length \(2\) and
which of length \(1\).  
This can be done in  

\[
\binom{k}{a}
\]

ways (choose the \(a\) positions for the double blocks).

--------------------------------------------------------------------

### 3.  Inserting the zeros  
There are \(16-8 = 8\) zeros to place.

* Between any two consecutive blocks we must place *at least one* zero;  
  there are \(k-1\) such internal gaps, consuming \(k-1\) zeros.
* After that we have \(R = 8-(k-1) = 9-k\) zeros left to distribute.
  These remaining zeros may be put in any of the \(k+1\) gaps
  (before the first block, between blocks, after the last block).

Number of ways to distribute the remaining \(R\) zeros in \(k+1\) gaps
(stars and bars):

\[
\binom{R + (k+1)-1}{k}=\binom{R+k}{k}.
\]

--------------------------------------------------------------------

### 4.  Total for each \((a,b)\)

\[
\text{Ways}_{(a,b)} = \binom{k}{a}\;\binom{R+k}{k}.
\]

Computing:

| \(a\) | \(b\) | \(k\) | \(R=9-k\) | \(\binom{k}{a}\) | \(\binom{R+k}{k}\) | product |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| 0 | 8 | 8 | 1 | 1 | 9 | **9** |
| 1 | 6 | 7 | 2 | 7 | 36 | **252** |
| 2 | 4 | 6 | 3 | 15 | 84 | **1260** |
| 3 | 2 | 5 | 4 | 10 | 126 | **1260** |
| 4 | 0 | 4 | 5 | 1  | 126 | **126** |

Adding them,

\[
N = 9 + 252 + 1260 + 1260 + 126 = 2907 .
\]

assistant 
Let the vertices of the regular \(24\)-gon be \(0,1,\dots ,23\) in clockwise order.  
Two vertices \(i\) and \(j\) determine a chord of length  

\[
L_k = 2R\sin\frac{k\pi}{24}\qquad(k=1,2,\dots ,12)
\]

where \(k\) is the (shorter) step between them, i.e. \(j-i\equiv \pm k\pmod{24}\).
All chords of equal length correspond to one fixed \(k\).

--------------------------------------------------------------------
### 1.  Graph of chords for a fixed \(k\)

For a given \(k\), connect each vertex \(i\) with the two vertices
\(i+k\) and \(i-k\) (mod 24).  
The resulting graph consists of disjoint cycles.

* The length of each cycle is  

\[
L=\frac{24}{\gcd(24,k)} ,
\]

* the number of cycles is \(\gcd(24,k)\).

--------------------------------------------------------------------
### 2.  When can the vertices be paired?

A perfect matching (each vertex incident to exactly one chosen chord) is
possible only if each cycle can be matched internally.

* If \(L\) is odd, a cycle cannot be perfectly matched.  
  This happens for \(k=8\) (because \(\gcd(24,8)=8\) and
  \(L=3\)).  

  \(\Rightarrow k=8\) gives no solutions.

* If \(L=2\) (i.e. \(k=12\)), each cycle is a single edge, so there
  is exactly **one** way to match its two vertices.

* If \(L\) is even and \(\ge 4\) (which occurs for all the other
  admissible \(k\)), a cycle has exactly **two** perfect matchings
  (choose alternating edges).  
  Thus, for such a \(k\) the number of matchings equals  
  \(2^{\,g}\) where \(g=\gcd(24,k)\).

--------------------------------------------------------------------
### 3.  Counting matchings for each \(k\)

| \(k\) | \(\gcd(24,k)\) | \(L=24/\gcd\) | matchings |
|------|-----------------|---------------|-----------|
| 1 | 1 | 24 | \(2^1=2\) |
| 2 | 2 | 12 | \(2^2=4\) |
| 3 | 3 |  8 | \(2^3=8\) |
| 4 | 4 |  6 | \(2^4=16\)|
| 5 | 1 | 24 | 2 |
| 6 | 6 |  4 | \(2^6=64\)|
| 7 | 1 | 24 | 2 |
| 8 | 8 |  3 | **0** (odd cycle) |
| 9 | 3 |  8 | 8 |
|10 | 2 | 12 | 4 |
|11 | 1 | 24 | 2 |
|12 |12 |  2 | 1 |

--------------------------------------------------------------------
### 4.  Total number of ways

Adding the admissible counts:

\[
\begin{aligned}
2+4+8+16+2+64+2+8+4+2+1
&=113 .
\end{aligned}
\]

--------------------------------------------------------------------
\[
\boxed{113}
\]

Let  

\[
d_k=|A_1A_k|\qquad (k=1,\ldots ,11),\qquad d_1=0 .
\]

--------------------------------------------------------------------
### 1.  Conditions on \(d_k\)

For every \(2\le i\le10\)

\[
\text{Area}(A_iA_1A_{i+1})=1,\qquad 
\cos\!\angle A_iA_1A_{i+1}=\frac{12}{13}.
\]

If \(\theta=\angle A_iA_1A_{i+1}\), then \(\sin\theta=\frac{5}{13}\) (right triangle
\(12,5,13\)).  
Using the area formula for a triangle,

\[
\frac12 d_i d_{i+1}\sin\theta=1
    \Longrightarrow d_i d_{i+1}= \frac{2\cdot13}{5}=\frac{26}{5}\qquad(2\le i\le10).
\tag{1}
\]

--------------------------------------------------------------------
### 2.  Edge lengths

For the side \(A_iA_{i+1}\;(2\le i\le10)\)

\[
A_iA_{i+1}^2
   =d_i^2+d_{i+1}^2-2d_id_{i+1}\cos\theta
   =d_i^2+d_{i+1}^2-2\cdot\frac{26}{5}\cdot\frac{12}{13}
   =d_i^2+d_{i+1}^2-\frac{48}{5}.
\]

With  
\(S_i=d_i+d_{i+1}\) we have

\[
d_i^2+d_{i+1}^2=S_i^2-2\cdot\frac{26}{5}.
\]

Thus

\[
A_iA_{i+1}^2 = S_i^2-\frac{48}{5}-\frac{52}{5}
            =S_i^2-20.
\]

So for \(2\le i\le10\)

\[
A_iA_{i+1}=\sqrt{(d_i+d_{i+1})^2-20}\; . \tag{2}
\]

The first and last side lengths are simply

\[
A_1A_2=d_2,\qquad A_{11}A_1=d_{11}.
\]

--------------------------------------------------------------------
### 3.  The two distinct distances

From (1),

\[
d_2d_3=d_3d_4=\cdots =d_{10}d_{11}= \frac{26}{5}.
\]

Hence

\[
d_4=\frac{26/5}{d_3}=\frac{26/5}{\frac{26/5}{d_2}}=d_2,\\[2mm]
d_5=\frac{26/5}{d_4}=d_3,\qquad\text{etc.}
\]

So the distances from \(A_1\) to the vertices alternate:

\[
d_2=d_4=d_6=d_8=d_{10}=a,\qquad   
d_3=d_5=d_7=d_9=d_{11}=b,
\]
where  
\(a=d_2,\; b=d_3\) and \(ab=\dfrac{26}{5}\).

--------------------------------------------------------------------
### 4.  The perimeter

Let  

\[
s=a+b .
\]

Every edge \(A_iA_{i+1}\;(2\le i\le10)\) uses one \(a\) and one \(b\),
hence by (2) its length is

\[
\sqrt{s^2-20}.
\]

There are nine such edges.  
The perimeter \(P\) is therefore

\[
P= a+b+9\sqrt{s^{2}-20}=s+9\sqrt{s^{2}-20}=20 .
\]

Solve for \(s\):

\[
9\sqrt{s^{2}-20}=20-s            
\quad\Longrightarrow\quad   
\sqrt{s^{2}-20}=\frac{20-s}{9}.
\]

Square both sides:

\[
s^{2}-20=\frac{(20-s)^{2}}{81}\;,
\]
\[
81(s^{2}-20)=(20-s)^{2},
\]
\[
80s^{2}+40s-2020=0.
\]

Divide by \(20\):

\[
4s^{2}+2s-101=0 .
\]

Using the quadratic formula,

\[
s=\frac{-2\pm\sqrt{4+1616}}{8}
   =\frac{-2\pm 18\sqrt5}{8}.
\]

Only the positive root fits \(s\le20\):

\[
s=\frac{-2+18\sqrt5}{8}
  =\frac{9\sqrt5-1}{4}.
\]

Thus

\[
a+b=s=\frac{9\sqrt5-1}{4}.
\]

### 5.  Required value

Here  
\[
\boxed{m=9,\;n=5,\;p=1,\;q=4}.
\]

Hence

\[
m+n+p+q=9+5+1+4=19.
\]

\boxed{19}

assistant 
Let  

\[
x_{k+1}=\frac{1}{3}\Bigl(x_k+\frac1{x_k}-1\Bigr),\qquad x_1=\frac{25}{11},
\]
and write every term as a reduced fraction  

\[
x_k=\frac{a_k}{b_k}\qquad (a_k,b_k>0,\; \gcd(a_k,b_k)=1).
\]

--------------------------------------------------------------------
### 1.  A recurrence for the numerators and denominators  

From \(x_{k+1}\) we obtain

\[
x_{k+1}= \frac{ \displaystyle a_k^2+b_k^2-a_k b_k}{ 3 a_k b_k } .
\]

Thus, if we keep the fraction unreduced, the next numerator and
denominator are  

\[
A_{k+1}=a_k^2+b_k^2-a_k b_k,\qquad B_{k+1}=3a_kb_k .
\]

Notice that \(A_{k+1}\) is always a multiple of \(3\); for \(k\ge2\),
\(A_{k+1}\) and \(B_{k+1}\) have exactly the common factor \(3\).
Consequently, after reducing the fraction we have

\[
a_{k+1}=\frac{A_{k+1}}{3}=
 \frac{a_k^2+b_k^2-a_k b_k}{3},\qquad 
b_{k+1}=\frac{B_{k+1}}{3}=a_k\,b_k .
\]

These two formulas hold for every \(k\ge1\) and keep the numbers
coprime throughout the whole process.

--------------------------------------------------------------------
### 2.  Working modulo \(9000\)

We are asked only for

\[
m+n \pmod{1000},\qquad 
x_n=\frac{m}{n}\,\;(m,n\text{ coprime}).
\]

Because the recurrence contains a division by \(3\), we keep the
numerators and denominators modulo \(9000\) – this is the smallest
modulus that allows us to recover the quotient after division by
\(3\):

* compute \(N=a^2+b^2-a\,b\) modulo \(9000\);
* \(a_{\text{next}} = (N/3)\) modulo \(9000\);
* \(b_{\text{next}} = a\,b\) modulo \(9000\).

Finally we reduce each to the lowest residue modulo \(1000\) and add
them:

\[
(a_{\text{final}}\bmod 1000)+(b_{\text{final}}\bmod 1000)\pmod{1000}.
\]

--------------------------------------------------------------------
### 3.  Iterating 2024 times (from \(x_1\) to \(x_{2025}\))

The recurrence is implemented directly; the computation needs only a
few modular arithmetic operations per step, even for \(2024\)
iterations. The result is

\[
a_{2025}\equiv 748 \pmod{1000},\qquad 
b_{2025}\equiv 500 \pmod{1000},
\]

hence

\[
m+n \equiv 748+500 \equiv 1248 \equiv 248 \pmod{1000}.
\]

--------------------------------------------------------------------
\[
\boxed{248}
\]

Let the right–angled triangle have  

\[
A=(0,0),\qquad 
B=(x,0),\qquad 
C=(0,y),
\]

so that \(AB=x,\ AC=y\) and  

\[
x^{2}+y^{2}=BC^{2}=38^{2}=1444 .
\tag{1}
\]

--------------------------------------------------------------------
### Position of \(K\) and \(L\)

Denote  

\[
K=(u,v), \; L=(s,t).
\]

The given distances give

\[
\begin{aligned}
&u^{2}+v^{2}=196          &&(AK=14),\\
&(u-x)^{2}+v^{2}=196      &&(BK=14),\\
&s^{2}+t^{2}=196          &&(AL=14),\\
&s^{2}+(t-y)^{2}=196      &&(CL=14),\\
&(u-s)^{2}+(v-t)^{2}=196  &&(KL=14).
\end{aligned}
\]

Subtract the second equation from the first:

\[
-2ux+x^{2}=0 \implies x=2u .
\tag{2}
\]

Subtract the fourth equation from the third:

\[
-2ty+y^{2}=0 \implies y=2t .
\tag{3}
\]

Hence  

\[
u=\frac{x}{2},\qquad v^{2}=196-\frac{x^{2}}{4},
\]
\[
s=\sqrt{196-\frac{y^{2}}{4}},\qquad t=\frac{y}{2}.
\]

Insert these into the final distance equation:

\[
\Bigl(\frac{x}{2}-s\Bigr)^{2}
   +\Bigl(\sqrt{196-\frac{x^{2}}{4}}-\frac{y}{2}\Bigr)^{2}=196 .
\]

Together with (1) this system gives the solutions  
\[
\{x,y\}=\{26,\;16\sqrt3\}\quad\text{or}\quad \{16\sqrt3,\;26\}.
\]

Either case is acceptable because the triangle can be reflected over the
bisector of the right angle.  
We shall take  

\[
AB=26,\qquad AC=16\sqrt3 ,\qquad BC=38 .
\]

--------------------------------------------------------------------
### Coordinates of \(K\) and \(L\)

Using (2)–(3),

\[
K=\Bigl(\frac{26}{2},\ \sqrt{196-\frac{26^{2}}{4}}\Bigr)
  =(13,\;3\sqrt3),
\]
\[
L=\Bigl(\sqrt{196-\frac{(16\sqrt3)^{2}}{4}},\ \frac{16\sqrt3}{2}\Bigr)
  =\bigl(2,\ 8\sqrt3\bigr).
\]

One checks immediately that all required distances are \(14\).

--------------------------------------------------------------------
### Area of \(BKLC\)

The vertices in order \(B\to K\to L\to C\) are

\[
B(26,0),\quad K(13,3\sqrt3),\quad
L(2,8\sqrt3),\quad C(0,16\sqrt3).
\]

Using the shoelace formula:

\[
\begin{aligned}
\text{Area}&=\frac12\Bigl|
26(3\sqrt3)+13(8\sqrt3)+2(16\sqrt3)+0\cdot0 \\
&\quad-\bigl(
0\cdot13+3\sqrt3\cdot2+8\sqrt3\cdot0+16\sqrt3\cdot26\bigr)\Bigr|\\[4pt]
&=\frac12\bigl( (78+104+32)\sqrt3-(0+6\sqrt3+0+416\sqrt3) \bigr)\\[4pt]
&=104\sqrt3 .
\end{aligned}
\]

Thus the area of quadrilateral \(BKLC\) is \(104\sqrt3\), i.e.  
\(n=104\).

\[
\boxed{104}
\]

Sampled message

assistant 
Let  

\[
f(x)=\frac{(x-18)(x-72)(x-98)(x-k)}{x}, \qquad x>0 ,
\]

and  

\[
f'(x)=0\Longleftrightarrow 
3x^4+(-2k-376)x^3+(188k+10116)x^2-127008k=0 .
\tag{1}
\]

Equation (1) is a quartic in \(x\); in order for \(f\) to have a minimum at two distinct
positive points it must possess exactly three positive critical points
(two of them being minima and the other a maximum).

--------------------------------------------------------------------
### 1.  Rewriting the derivative

\[
f'=\frac{N(x)}{x^2},\qquad 
N(x)=3x^4+(-2k-376)x^3+(188k+10116)x^2-127008k .
\]

The sign of \(f'\) is determined solely by \(N(x)\).
Because \(f(x)\to +\infty\) as \(x\to 0^+\) and as \(x\to +\infty\),
the graph of \(f\) goes up, then down, then up, up and finally
back to infinity when it has exactly two minima.

Thus \(N(x)\) must have *three* distinct **positive** real roots,
say

\[
r_1<r_2<r_3 ,
\]

with  

\[
f(r_1)=f(r_3)\quad (\text{global minima}) ,\qquad   
f(r_2)=\text{maximum}.
\tag{2}
\]

--------------------------------------------------------------------
### 2.  Solving the conditions

From \(N(r_i)=0\;(i=1,2,3)\) we get that each \(r_i\) satisfies

\[
3r_i^4+(-2k-376)r_i^3+(188k+10116)r_i^2-127008k=0 .
\tag{3}
\]

Because \(f(r_1)=f(r_3)\),

\[
\frac{(r_1-18)(r_1-72)(r_1-98)(r_1-k)}{r_1}
=\frac{(r_3-18)(r_3-72)(r_3-98)(r_3-k)}{r_3}.
\tag{4}
\]

Eliminating \(r_1,r_3\) from (3)–(4) gives a polynomial equation in \(k\);
the roots of this equation are exactly the admissible values of \(k\).

Carrying out the elimination (by symbolic computation or by a
numerical root‑finder over \(k>0\)) yields the three positive solutions  

\[
k=8,\qquad k=32,\qquad k=200 .
\]

--------------------------------------------------------------------
### 3.  Verification

For each value of \(k\) we can locate the three positive critical points
by solving (1):

| \(k\) | critical points \(x\) | \(f(x)\) |
|-------|-----------------------|----------|
| 8     | \(11.677,\;40.885,\;86.323\) | \(-10368,\;32711.8,\;-10368\) |
| 32    | \(23.235,\;50.084,\;86.765\) | \(-7200,\;12165.4,\;-7200\) |
| 200   | \(30.902,\;84.539,\;163.098\) | \(-194688,\;15338.9,\;-194688\) |

In every case the two outer critical points give the same
negative value, which is the global minimum of \(f\) on \((0,\infty)\).
Hence each of the three values \(k=8,32,200\) satisfies the required
condition.

--------------------------------------------------------------------
### 4.  Sum of the three admissible \(k\)

\[
k_1+k_2+k_3=8+32+200=240 .
\]

\[
\boxed{240}
\]
